O you who have believed, be staunch in justice, witnesses for Allah, even if it be against yourselves or parents and relatives. Whether one is rich or poor,
Allah is more worthy of both. So follow not [personal] inclination, lest you not be just.
And if you distort your testimony or refuse to give it, then indeed Allah is aware of what you do, and he is well informed of your actions

Returning to the theme of mercy and compassion, the following passage from his letter is remarkable in placing the necessity of compassion in a universal context. It serves as one of the most important expressions by Maulana Ali on the unity of the human race and the equality of all human beings. It stands forth as a corrective guide to all forms of prejudice that would apply justice or compassion only to members of one's own 'group' however they define it. He says:
وَأَشْعِرْ قَلْبَكَ الرَّحْمَةَ لِلرَّعِيَّةِ، وَالْـمَحَبَّةَ لَهُمْ، وَاللُّطْفَ بِهِمْ

Infuse your heart with mercy for your subjects, and have affection and kindness for them.
وَلاَ تَكُونَنَّ عَلَيْهِمْ سَبُعاً ضَارِياً تَغْتَنِمُ أَكْلَهُمْ

Do not stand over them like greedy beasts who feel it is fair game to devour them
فَإِنَّهُمْ صِنْفَانِ: إِمَّا أَخٌ لَكَ فِي الدِّينِ، وَإمّا نَظِيرٌ لَكَ فِي الْخَلْقِ، يَفْرُطُ مِنْهُمُ الزَّلَلُ، وَتَعْرِضُ لَهُمُ الْعِلَلُ،يُؤْتَى عَلَى أَيْدِيهِمْ فِي الَعَمْدِ وَالْخَطَاَ، فَأَعْطِهِمْ مِنْ عَفْوِكَ وَصَفْحِكَ مِثْلَ الَّذِي تُحِبُّ أَنْ يُعْطِيَكَ اللهُ مِنْ عَفْوِهِ وَصَفْحِهِ

They are of two kinds. They are either your brothers in religion or they are similar to you in creation. They will slip from time to time and make mistakes. They may act wrongly, willingly or by neglect. So, extend to them your forgiveness and pardon, in the same way as you would like Allah to extend His forgiveness and pardon to you.
فَإِنَّكَ فَوْقَهُمْ، وَ وَالِي الاْمْرِ عَلَيْكَ فَوْقَكَ، وَاللهُ فَوْقَ مَنْ وَلاَّكَ! وَقَدِ اسْتَكْفَاكَ أَمْرَهُمْ، وَابْتَلاَكَ بِهِمْ

For you are responsible for them; and your Commander (Imam) is responsible for you; while Allah is over him who has appointed you. Allah has sought you to manage their affairs and has tried you through them.

So you can see that the universal application of compassion here, is married to a reminder of the absolute sovereignty of Allah. No man, whether he is a governor, or the ruler appointing the governor, is anything but the slave of Allah. Everyone, regardless of their position of power is utterly and completely dependent upon Allah's mercy. Therefore **every person who finds himself in a position of relative superiority over others, must constantly remember his inferiority in relation to the Absolute**. This awareness will help him be compassionate towards those under him; and it will attract for himself the compassion of Allah who is above him.

The author cites and incident here that not only demonstrates Maulana Ali's compassionate implementation of justice, it also serves as an expression of this principle, that all men are similar to you in their creation. Maulana Ali once came across an old, blind beggar who was in a horrible state. When he inquired about him, he was told that the beggar was a Christian. He said to those around him, 'You have employed this man to the point where he is old and infirm, and now you refuse to help him?! Give him money from the public funds (⟪ar:بیت المال⟫) so that he can sustain himself .

Now isn't it interesting that when it came to his own brother (old, blind and poor like this Christian beggar). Someone who does not only share the same religion, but also his same parents - his own flesh and blood! He refused to give him the funds, but when it came to this old man following a different religion, he commanded that he is provided from the public funds. Here we can see such a starch distinction, from the blatant and shameless nepotism that was displayed by ⟪ar:عثمان⟫where he appointed all his relatives as governors. Many of whom were well-known hypocrites.

But in addition to all this, Maulana Ali's command succinctly expresses the Islamic principle of social welfare (⟪ar:مصلحۃ⟫) - based on a policy of strict non-discrimination between Muslims and others. We can see that in his decisions, social justice, and religious equality, they flow from the sentiment of compassion; as an intrinsic dimension of the Real. His decision to grant funds to the old blind Christian was purely out of compassion while enacting justice - putting things in the right place. Because this person had no other means of sustaining himself other than begging. Whereas when it came to his own blind brother, his refusal was out of enacting justice as well. Because ⟪ar:عقیل⟫was asking him to abuse his powers and authority. And by giving him a taste of the heat of hellfire, he saved him for hellfire itself - that was also out of his compassion for his brother. You see that?

So this then translates into a fundamental religious duty and obligation upon all Muslims, rulers, citizens, and us. We have to exhibit compassion in our dealings with the people we have authority over, while trying our best to enact justice. Only then we will succeed in aligning our nature with the divine.

The final sentence of the passage is very interesting. He says:
وَقَدِ اسْتَكْفَاكَ أَمْرَهُمْ، وَابْتَلاَكَ بِهِمْ

That you should regard your power over your subjects not simply as a privilege, but more as a trial from Allah. This clearly expresses his own paradigm of how he viewed his own ruler-ship.

In one of his famous sermons known as ⟪ar:خطبۃ الشقشقیۃ⟫, he says that he accepted power, not because he desired it, but as an unavoidable duty. He says:
وَالَّذِي فَلَقَ الْحَبَّةَ، وَبَرَأَ النَّسَمَةَ،

By the one who split the grain to grow, and created living beings
لَوْلاَ حُضُورُ الْحَاضِرِ، وَقِيَامُ الْحُجَّةِ بِوُجُودِ النَّاصِرِ، وَمَا أَخَذَ اللهُ عَلَى العُلَمَاءِ أَلاَّ يُقَارُّوا عَلَى كِظَّةِ ظَالِم، وَلا سَغَبِ مَظْلُوم

If people had not come to me and supporters had not exhausted their argument; and if there wasn't the pledge of Allah with the learned, that they should not accept the gluttony of the oppressor, and the hunger of the oppressed,
لأَلقَيْتُ حَبْلَهَا عَلَى غَارِبِهَا

I would have cast the rope of Caliphate back on its shoulders.
وَلَسَقَيْتُ آخِرَهَا بِكَأْسِ أَوَّلِها

I would have treated the last one similar to the first.
وَلأَلْفَيْتُمْ دُنْيَاكُمْ هذِهِ أَزْهَدَ عِنْدِي مِنْ عَفْطَةِ عَنْز

Then you would have seen that in my view this world of yours is as insignificant to me as that which drips from the nose of a goat. SubhanAllah!

We can clearly see here that in Maulana Ali's view, the true ruler is the one who rules only for the sake of people. Who does not regard his authority as a distinction, but rather sees it as an unavoidable task; focusing only on the truth, considering justice as the one indispensable thing in all his acts of service. Only such a person can bring prosperity to the group he rules over.

We learn that the principle underlying this approach to authority and governance (no matter how large or small the subjected group is) - it is the responsibility of privileged, to act with generosity and nobility toward those that are less privileged. If you have been granted Allah's grace; if you have been blessed with the privilege of knowledge - a great responsibility falls upon you. The responsibility of justice. To put things in their proper place. To put compassion where it belongs - with the less fortunate. Because your duty to Allah increases, in proportion to the graces he has bestowed upon you. In one of his sermons, Maulana Ali said: The blessings of Allah upon a person do not become immense without the right of Allah over him becoming immense.

So we should all really take a look to recognize and appreciate the blessings of Allah in our lives, and then understand our responsibility, and our role in enacting justice with those who are less fortunate. So many people are always thinking in terms of what it's his job, or his job. It's the leaders job, the head of the state's job - What about your and my job. What about our responsibilities in trying to fix things that we have control over? May Allah guide us all to be just and help us all align with his beautiful nature.

<!-- section 19 (id=208, raw_sort=19): Good Opinion Of Allah -->

# A good opinion of Allah

We now come to a subtle point in letter, where Maulana Ali instructs ⟪ar:مالک⟫on the kind of advisers he should have close to him. He tells him to avoid misers and cowards. He says
وَلاَ تُدْخِلَنَّ فِي مَشُورَتِكَ بَخِيلاً يَعْدِلُ بِكَ عَنِ الْفَضْلِ، وَيَعِدُكَ الْفَقْرَ، وَلاَ جَبَاناً يُضعِّفُكَ عَنِ الاْمُورِ، وَلاَ حَرِيصاً يُزَيِّنُ لَكَ الشَّرَهَ بِالْجَوْرِ، فَإِنَّ الْبُخْلَ وَالْجُبْنَ وَالْحِرْصَ غَرَائِزُ شَتَّى يَجْمَعُهَا سُوءُ الظَّنِّ بِاللهِ. 

Do not include among those you consult a miser who would hold you back from being generous, and caution you against destitution. Nor a coward, who would make you feel too weak for your affairs; nor a greedy person who would make the collection of wealth by evil ways beautiful to you. This is because miserliness, cowardice and greed are vices that are brought together by an unfavorable opinion of Allah.

The phrase⟪ar:سُوءُ الظَّنِّ بِاللهِ⟫ "a bad opinion of Allah" evokes the idea of a misunderstanding of the divine nature. When you misunderstand the divine nature, it gives rise to such negative qualities. The miser has the bad opinion that Allah might not provide in the future. The coward thinks that Allah might not protect. The greedy believes that Allah has provided less than what he needs. So from this point of view, all human vices stem from an intellectual dysfunction, which is the consequence of a weak faith. Atheism is a severe form of dysfunction, because an outright disbelief in Allah is a denial of Allah's reality, therefore a kind of 'bad opinion' about Him.

Now the logical opposite of this would be, that a strong and deep faith in the reality of Allah and a proper understanding of the divine nature would generate a 'good opinion' of Allah (⟪ar:حسن الظن⟫). This good opinion or perception of the divine Reality would in turn leads to the soul being impressed by the divine qualities comprised within that Reality. You see? Because these divine qualities should not only be viewed as transcendent - meaning some quality that is absolutely one with the divine essence and utterly beyond the reach of the human soul - No! They are to be grasped as role models, as exemplars, as patterns or perfections for the soul to emulate to whatever degree is possible.

Now does this mean that in doing so we are comparing ourselves to Allah in greatness? No. The goal is to be as much 'like' Allah as possible, but this spiritual development can only take place within the context of one's servitude to Him. In other words, one must be generous, for the sake of Allah; because Allah is ⟪ar:الکریم⟫- The Generous. One should be compassionate and merciful because Allah is compassionate and merciful - because he is ⟪ar:الرحمن الرحیم⟫ and so on. Not because I am generous, or I am merciful or I am compassionate. It's emulating the qualities of Allah, not personifying him. We must remain lowly, humble and effaced before Allah, while emulating his characteristics. With the understanding that no amount of virtue or intelligence can diminish or equate the qualities of Allah. There can never be a relation between the Absolute and the relative; between the creator and the created.

All our virtues, such as humility and effacement are simply modes through which we express our absolute dependence upon Allah. They are means of manifesting the transcendence of the Absolute. Thus, there is this paradox that exists between the human nature and the divine. On one hand we must try to be like Allah by adopting his character - as he said, "He who does not show mercy to creation, will not receive mercy from the creator". But at the same time, realize the very opposite of that
يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّاسُ أَنتُمُ ٱلْفُقَرَآءُ إِلَى ٱللَّهِۖ وَٱللَّهُ هُوَ ٱلْغَنِىُّ ٱلْحَمِيدُ

O mankind, you are the destitute before Allah, and Allah is self-sufficient and absolutely Praiseworthy. There must, therefore, be a synthesis between the active human virtues and ones spiritual consciousness. The human virtues as positive reflections of the divine qualities, and spiritual consciousness of one's nothingness before the divine Reality.

This is similar to looking into a mirror - where you are seeing yourself, but what you're seeing is an inverted reflection. When looking in the divine mirror, the goal is to see an inverted reflection of the divine transcendence on the human plane. On one hand we are ⟪ar:خلیفۃ اللہ⟫- the Caliphs and representatives of Allah on this earth; and on the other, simultaneously, we are his ⟪ar:عباد اللہ⟫- we are the slaves of Allah.

This consciousness of our servitude ensures that while we're representing Allah, we do not subconsciously slip into competing with Him in grandeur. Whereas our active commitment to represent Allah's attributes keeps reminding us of the dignity and responsibilities that are obligatory upon us as human beings. Because without that, we would fall into a state of spiritual paralysis; and neglect our duty towards the world. The equilibrium and balance between these two dimensions of human existence is another aspect of sacred justice, of putting things in their right place; and it also attunes the quality of moral action to the harmony of pure being.

# State and society

The letter then continues were Maulana Ali then gives a description of the different classes of people within state and society. He talks about the army, the scribes, the judiciary bodies, executive officers, tax-collectors, peasants, merchants, artisans and, finally, the destitute. He talks about each class having its particular rights and duties. The instructions that he gives in this part of the letter can be regarded as the cutting-edge of justice for all practical purposes. It takes into account all aspects of a social and political life.

Malik as the governor is commanded to regard each of these classes under his authority as having rights and obligations. But the essential feature of the governor's relationship to society as a whole, is again determined by his primary duty to Allah. This is why he says:
وَكُلٌّ قَدْ سَمَّى اللهُ سَهْمَهُ، وَوَضَعَ عَلَى حَدِّهِ وَفَرِيضَتِهِ فِي كِتَابِهِ أَوْ سُنَّةِ نَبِيِّهِ (صلى الله عليه وآله)عَهْداً مِنْهُ عِنْدَنَا مَحْفُوظاً 

Allah has ordained the share of every one of them and laid down His covenants about the limits of each in His Book and the ⟪ar:سنۃ⟫of His Prophet by way of of a settlement which is preserved with us.

So the governors duty to society is part of the universal covenant between man and Allah - the ⟪ar:عہد⟫that is so frequently encountered in the Quran. Allah says:

The Thunder [13:19-20]
[ سورۃ ۱۳] الرعد

‏ أَفَمَن يَعْلَمُ أَنَّمَآ أُنزِلَ إِلَيْكَ مِن رَّبِّكَ ٱلْحَقُّ كَمَنْ هُوَ أَعْمَىٰٓ ۚ إِنَّمَا يَتَذَكَّرُ أُو۟لُوا۟ ٱلْأَلْبَبِ ‎۱۹‏ٱلَّذِينَ يُوفُونَ بِعَهْدِ ٱللَّهِ وَلَا يَنقُضُونَ ٱلْمِيثَقَ ‎۲۰
( 19 ) Then is he who knows that what has been revealed to you from your Lord is the truth like one who is blind? Only people of true understanding will reflect on this( 20 ) Those who fulfill the covenant of Allah and do not break the contract, 

The Children of Israel [17:34]
[ سورۃ ۱۷] الاسرا

‏وَلَا تَقْرَبُوا۟ مَالَ ٱلْيَتِيمِ إِلَّا بِٱلَّتِى هِىَ أَحْسَنُ حَتَّىٰ يَبْلُغَ أَشُدَّهُۥ ۚ وَأَوْفُوا۟ بِٱلْعَهْدِ ۖ إِنَّ ٱلْعَهْدَ كَانَ مَسْـُٔولًۭا ‎۳۴
And do not approach the property of an orphan, except in the way that is best, until he reaches maturity. And fulfill [every] commitment. Indeed, the commitment is ever [that about which one will be] questioned.

Also, immediately prior to these instructions, Maulana Ali advises Malik
وَأَكْثِرْ مُدَارَسَةَ الَعُلَمَاءِ، وَمُنَافَثَةَ الْحُكَمَاءِ، فِي تَثْبِيتِ مَا صَلَحَ عَلَيْهِ أَمْرُ بِلاَدِكَ، وَإِقَامَةِ مَا اسْتَقَامَ بِهِ النَّاسُ قَبْلَكَ

He advises Malik to deeply study the ⟪ar:علماء⟫the scholars, and to hold much discourse with the ⟪ar:حکما⟫the sages. He says, you should do that in order to consolidate that which brings well-being to your lands, and to further strengthen what has already been established by your predecessors. Now what's the difference between scholars and sages? The difference is that of the formal rules as established in the Quran and ⟪ar:سنۃ⟫, and the spirit behind them. The scholars (the ⟪ar:علماء⟫) are the ones who have the knowledge of the ⟪ar:شریعۃ⟫, whereas the sages, the ⟪ar:حکما⟫know how to apply that knowledge. That is the spirit of wisdom. Wisdom is the application of knowledge. You have the letter of the law (managed by the scholars) and then there is the spirit of the law, which is the domain of the sages. Without wisdom, one cannot apply formal rules with care and sensitivity.

Example of the woman who gave herself up for water

So Maulana Ali advises ⟪ar:مالک⟫to keep in constant communication with both the scholars as well as the sages. The scholars will keep him connected with the formalities of religion, whereas the sages will help him use and apply those formalities in the correct manner.

Then he begins describing the interdependence of each class or segment of society. He begins with the underpinning of the state by the soldiers, and ends with the governor himself being the center around which the different elements revolve. It's literally like the wheel. It works on the exact same principles. A wheel has a center with spokes coming out of it that connect to the tire. But the center itself is connected to the axle which makes it rotate. So the axle empowers and moves the center of the wheel, and the wheel rotates the tire. The governor is that center, working on the horizontal plane working with creation. The spokes are the various mentioned classes of people. But this horizontal center is nothing without the vertical axle of the creator. Just as the center of the wheel is useless without its connection with the axle, all the efforts of the governor are useless without his connection with the Real. So essentially all other classes of people: the army, the  secretarial workers, the chiefs, the dispensers of justice, the tax collectors, the traders, the men of industry, and the lowest class of the needy and the destitute - they all revolve around the center - which is the governor of the state , and the governor himself must be connected with the axle of Allah. He must be completely dependent on the vertical axis - which is Allah(Subhanahu wa Ta'ala). Only then will a society truly prosper.

<!-- section 20 (id=210, raw_sort=20): Classes Of People -->

## The Army

Then Maulana Ali provides instructions pertaining to the different categories of state and society. He begins with the army. He says:
فَوَلِّ مِنْ جُنُودِكَ أَنْصَحَهُمْ فِي نَفْسِكَ لله وَلِرَسُولِهِ وَلاِمَامِكَ، وَأَنْقَاهُمْ جَيْباً، وَأَفْضَلَهُمْ حِلْماً مِمَّنْ يُبْطِىءُ عَنِ الْغَضَبِ، وَيَسْتَرِيحُ إِلَى الْعُذْرِ، وَيَرْأَفُ بِالضُّعَفَاءِ، وَيَنْبُو عَلَى الاْقْوِيَاءِ، وَمِمَّنْ لاَ يُثِيرُهُ الْعُنْفُ، وَلاَ يَقْعُدُ بِهِ الضَّعْفُ. 

For the position of commander of the troops, Malik is told to select a person who is the most sincere in relation to Allah, and his Prophet and your Imam. Who is the purest of heart, the one most excellent in forbearance (حلم), who is slow to anger, happy to pardon, kind to the weak, severe with the strong; one who is neither moved by violence, nor held back by weakness. SubhanAllah! What a perfect description of the ideal commander! This description also reflects Maulana Ali's own characters.

Now isn't it interesting that generally these qualities of kindness, gentleness and forbearance, these aren't qualities that are typically not associated with fighting men. So here in these statements we observe a spiritually refined attitude towards an activity which, if left to its own devices, would wreck havoc and ruin all norms of decency and propriety. This is why historically when a nation defeats another nation, what happens? Rape, killing of citizens, pillaging. This is because the نفس الامارۃ is at its worst in that situation. The intoxication of power drives soldiers to complete madness.

So Maulana Ali 's description of the perfect warrior is the one whose natural disposition is 'spirited' and courageous, possessing the quality of being gentle to their own people and dangerous only to their enemies. This echoes Quranic description of

Victory [48:29]
[ سورۃ ۴۸] الفتح

‏مُّحَمَّدٌۭ رَّسُولُ ٱللَّهِ ۚ وَٱلَّذِينَ مَعَهُۥٓ أَشِدَّآءُ عَلَى ٱلْكُفَّارِ رُحَمَآءُ بَيْنَهُمْ ۖ تَرَىٰهُمْ رُكَّعًۭا سُجَّدًۭا يَبْتَغُونَ فَضْلًۭا مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ وَرِضْوَنًۭا ۖ سِيمَاهُمْ فِى وُجُوهِهِم مِّنْ أَثَرِ ٱلسُّجُودِ ۚ ذَلِكَ مَثَلُهُمْ فِى ٱلتَّوْرَىٰةِ ۚ وَمَثَلُهُمْ فِى ٱلْإِنجِيلِ كَزَرْعٍ أَخْرَجَ شَطْـَٔهُۥ فَـَٔازَرَهُۥ فَٱسْتَغْلَظَ فَٱسْتَوَىٰ عَلَىٰ سُوقِهِۦ يُعْجِبُ ٱلزُّرَّاعَ لِيَغِيظَ بِهِمُ ٱلْكُفَّارَ ۗ وَعَدَ ٱللَّهُ ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّلِحَتِ مِنْهُم مَّغْفِرَةًۭ وَأَجْرًا عَظِيمًۢا ‎۲۹
Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah ; and those with him are forceful against the disbelievers, merciful among themselves. You see them bowing and prostrating [in prayer], seeking bounty from Allah and [His] pleasure. Their mark is on their faces from the trace of prostration. That is their description in the Torah. And their description in the Gospel is as a plant which produces its offshoots and strengthens them so they grow firm and stand upon their stalks, delighting the sowers - so that Allah may enrage by them the disbelievers. Allah has promised those who believe and do righteous deeds among them forgiveness and a great reward.

So for the commander of the troops, Maulana Ali emphasis on the gentle qualities required in a warrior, as the essential complement to his martial virtues.

## The Judges

Turning to the next category, the judges, Maulana Ali says:
ثُمَّ اخْتَرْ لِلْحُكْمِ بَيْنَ النَّاسِ أَفْضَلَ رَعِيَّتِكَ فِي نَفْسِكَ، مِمَّنْ لاَ تَضِيقُ بِهِ الاْمُورُ، وَلاَ تُمَحِّكُهُ الْخُصُومُ، وَلاَ يَتَمادَى فِي الزَّلَّةِ، وَلاَ يَحْصَرُمِنَ الْفَيْءِ إِلَى الْحَقِّ إذَا عَرَفَهُ، وَلاَ تُشْرِفُ نَفْسُهُ عَلَى طَمَع، وَلاَ يَكْتَفِي بِأَدْنَى فَهْم دُونَ أَقصَاهُ، أَوْقَفَهُمْ فِي الشُّبُهَاتِ، وَآخَذَهُمْ بِالْحُجَجِ، وَأَقَلَّهُمْ تَبَرُّماً بِمُرَاجَعَةِ الْخَصْمِ، وَأَصْبَرَهُمْ عَلَى تَكَشُّفِ الاْمُورِ، وَأَصْرَمَهُمْ عِنْدَ اتِّضَاحِ الْحُكْمِ، مِمَّنْ لاَ يَزْدَهِيهِ إطْرَاءٌ، وَلاَ يَسْتَمِيلُهُ إِغْرَاءٌ،أُولئِكَ قَلِيلٌ

For the settlement of disputes among people select him who is the most distinguished of your subjects in your view. لاَ تَضِيقُ بِهِ الاْمُورُ They must not be confused by complexities; وَلاَ تُمَحِّكُهُ الْخُصُومُ they should not be enraged by disputes; وَلاَ يَتَمادَى فِي الزَّلَّةِ he should not agree on anything wrong; وَلاَ يَحْصَرُمِنَ الْفَيْءِ إِلَى الْحَقِّ إذَا عَرَفَهُ he must be unflinching in the face of the truth; لاَ تُشْرِفُ نَفْسُهُ عَلَى طَمَع he must be free from greed; وَلاَ يَكْتَفِي بِأَدْنَى فَهْم دُونَ أَقصَاهُ he must remain dissatisfied with superficial solutions, أَوْقَفَهُمْ فِي الشُّبُهَاتِ he should take the time to ponder over doubtful matters; وَآخَذَهُمْ بِالْحُجَجِ he should be consequential in argumentation; وَأَقَلَّهُمْ تَبَرُّماً بِمُرَاجَعَةِ الْخَصْمِ; he should not get disgusted at the quarrel of litigants; وَأَصْبَرَهُمْ عَلَى تَكَشُّفِ الاْمُورِ He should be patient when probing into matters; وَأَصْرَمَهُمْ عِنْدَ اتِّضَاحِ الْحُكْمِ ; he must be fearless when passing judgment; مِمَّنْ لاَ يَزْدَهِيهِ إطْرَاءٌ، وَلاَ يَسْتَمِيلُهُ إِغْرَاءٌ ; Praise should not make him vain and elation should not lead him to prejudice. And then he says أُولئِكَ قَلِيلٌ 'Such people' are indeed rare.

## Executive Officers

Regarding the administrators, Malik is told
ثُمَّ انْظُرْ فِي أُمُورِ عُمَّالِكَ، فَاسْتَعْمِلْهُمُ اخْتِبَاراً، وَلاَ تُوَلِّهِمْ مُحَابَاةً وأَثَرَةً، فَإِنَّهُمَا جِمَاعٌ مِنْ شُعَبِ الْجَوْرِ وَالْخِيَانَةِ. وَتوَخَّ مِنْهُمْ أَهْلَ التَّجْرِبَةِ وَالْحَيَاءِ، مِنْ أَهْلِ الْبُيُوتَاتِ الصَّالِحَةِ، وَالْقَدَمِ فِي الاْسْلاَمِ الْمُتَقَدِّمَةِ، فَإِنَّهُمْ أَكْرَمُ أَخْلاَقاً، وَأَصَحُّ أَعْرَاضاً، وَأَقَلُّ فِي الْمَطَامِعِ إِشْرَافاً، وَأَبْلَغُ فِي عَوَاقِبِ الاْمُورِ نَظَراً

Look into the affairs of your executives. Appointment them only after testing them, and not out of partiality or favoritism, because they constitute sources of injustice and unfairness. Select from among them those who are people of experience and modesty, hailing from virtuous houses, having been previously in Islam, because such people possess high manners and untarnished honor. They are the least inclined towards greed and always have their eyes on the final goal of matters. He says:
ثُمَّ أَسْبِغْ عَلَيْهِمُ الاْرْزَاقَ، فَإِنَّ ذلِكَ قُوَّةٌ لَهُمْ عَلَى اسْتِصْلاَحِ أَنْفُسِهِمْ، وَغِنىً لَهُمْ عَنْ تَنَاوُلِ مَا تَحْتَ أَيْدِيهِمْ، وَحُجَّةٌ عَلَيْهِمْ إِنْ خَالَفُوا أَمْرَكَ أَوْ ثَلَمُوا أَمَانَتَكَ 

Give them an abundant livelihood because this gives them the strength to maintain themselves in order, and not have an eye upon the funds in their custody. This would also be an argument against them if they disobeyed your orders or betrayed your trust. But then he also says:
فَإِنْ أَحَدٌ مِنْهُمْ بَسَطَ يَدَهُ إِلَى خِيَانَة اجْتَمَعَتْ بِهَا عَلَيْهِ عِنْدَكَ أَخْبَارُ عُيُونِكَ، اكْتَفَيْتَ بِذلِكَ شَاهِداً، فَبَسَطْتَ عَلَيْهِ الْعُقُوبَةَ فِي بَدَنِهِ، وَأَخَذْتَهُ بِمَا أَصَابَ مِنْ عَمَلِهِ، ثُمَّ نَصَبْتَهُ بِمَقَامِ الْمَذَلَّةِ، وَ وَسَمْتَهُ بِالْخِيانَةِ، وَقَلَّدْتَهُ عَارَ التُّهَمَةِ. 

If any one of them extends his hands towards misappropriation, and the reports reaching you confirm it, that should be regarded as enough evidence. You should then inflict corporal punishment on him and recover what he has misappropriated. You should put him in a place of disgrace, blacklist him with the charge of misappropriation, and make him wear the necklace of shame for his offence.

## Administration of Revenues

Regarding these administrators, he says: Look after the revenue affairs in such a way that those engaged in it remain prosperous because in their prosperity lies the prosperity of everyone. The others classes cannot prosper without them, because they are all dependent on revenue and its payers. But then in relation to those tilling the soil, and from whom the land tax is collected, Maulana Ali dispenses this wise maxim
وَلْيَكُنْ نَظَرُكَ فِي عِمَارَةِ الاْرْضِ أَبْلَغَ مِنْ نَظَرِكَ فِي اسْتِجْلاَبِ الْخَرَاجِ، لاِنَّ ذلِكَ لاَ يُدْرَكُ إِلاَّ بَالْعِمَارَةِ، وَمَنْ طَلَبَ الْخَرَاجَ بِغَيْرِ عِمَارَة أَخْرَبَ الْبِلاَدَ، وَأَهْلَكَ الْعِبَادَ، وَلَمْ يَسْتَقِمْ أَمْرُهُ إِلاَّ قَلِيلاً 

You should also keep an eye on the cultivation of the land more than on the collection of revenue because revenue cannot be generated without cultivation and whoever asks for revenue without cultivation, ruins the area and brings death to the people. Such a persons rule will not last only a moment. He says:
فَإِنْ شَكَوْا ثِقَلاً أَوْ عِلَّةً، أَوِ انْقِطَاعَ شِرْب أَوْ بَالَّة، أَوْ إِحَالَةَ أَرْض اغْتَمَرَهَا غَرَقٌ، أَوْ أَجْحَفَ بِهَا عَطَشٌ، خَفَّفْتَ عَنْهُمْ بِما تَرْجُو أَنْ يصْلُحَ بِهِ أَمْرُهُمْ، وَلاَ يَثْقُلَنَّ عَلَيْكَ شَيْءٌ خَفَّفْتَ بِهِ الْمَؤُونَةَ عَنْهُمْ، فَإِنَّهُ ذُخْرٌ يَعُودُونَ بِهِ عَلَيْكَ فِي عِمَارَةِ بِلادِكَ، وَتَزْيِينِ وِلاَيَتِكَ، مَعَ اسْتِجْلاَبِكَ حُسْنَ ثَنَائِهِمْ، وَتَبَجُّحِكَ بِاسْتِفَاضَةِ الْعَدْلِ فِيهِمْ 

If they complain of the burdens of tax, or of diseases, or shortage of water, or excess of water, or of a change in the condition of the land either due to flood or drought, you should remit the taxes to the extent that you hope will improve their situation. This remission granted for the removal of distress from them should not be grudged by you, because it is an investment which they will return to you in the shape of the prosperity of your country, and the progress of your domain - in addition to earning their praise and happiness for carrying out justice with them.
وَإِنَّمَا يُؤْتَى خَرَابُ الاْرْضِ مِنْ إِعْوَازِ أَهْلِهَا،إِنَّمَا يُعْوِزُ أَهْلُهَا لاِشْرَافِ أَنْفُسِ الْوُلاَةِ عَلَى الْجَمْعِ، وَسُوءِ ظَنِّهِمْ بِالْبَقَاءِ، وَقِلَّةِ انْتِفَاعِهِمْ بِالْعِبَرِ

The devastation of the land only comes through the destitution of its inhabitants ; and the destitution of its inhabitants only comes about when the desire to amass wealth rules the souls of the governors; when they have doubts about what endures, and when they profit little from exemplary teachings.

Regarding the phrase "having doubts about what endures" commentators state that this could either mean that the governors think that they will endure forever, forgetting that they will die and be judged in the Hereafter; or that they doubt that their position will endure for long, and hence exploit the opportunities available to them while they can.

For our learning, the first explanation is the more suitable to grasp, given the extent to which it echoes the Quranic verses

The Traducer [104:1-4]
[ سورۃ ۱۰۴] الحمزۃ

‏وَيْلٌۭ لِّكُلِّ هُمَزَةٍۢ لُّمَزَةٍ ‎۱‏ٱلَّذِى جَمَعَ مَالًۭا وَعَدَّدَهُۥ ‎۲‏يَحْسَبُ أَنَّ مَالَهُۥٓ أَخْلَدَهُۥ ‎۳‏كَلَّا ۖ لَيُنۢبَذَنَّ فِى ٱلْحُطَمَةِ ‎۴
( 1 ) Woe to every slanderer and backbiter.  ( 2 ) Who has gathered wealth and counted it,  ( 3 ) He thinks that his wealth will make him last forever! ( 4 ) Nay! Verily, he will be thrown into the crushing Fire. 

These verses could be considered as عبرا, the exemplary teaching'referred to by Maulana Ali. Also he uses the word جمع for amassing of wealth

⟪ar:إِنَّمَا يُعْوِزُ أَهْلُهَا لاِشْرَافِ أَنْفُسِ الْوُلاَةِ عَلَى الْجَمْعِ⟫

The same word is used in this verse  that is found in this verse.

⟪ar:‏وَيْلٌۭ لِّكُلِّ هُمَزَةٍۢ لُّمَزَةٍ ٱلَّذِى جَمَعَ مَالًۭا وَعَدَّدَهُ⟫

So the lesson or the teaching is clear. Greed is a symptom of a spiritual nearsightedness. Forgetting what truly matters, and what truly endures, leads to the various degrees of greed. So greed in this context is the desire for amassing the illusory material riches, instead of an everlasting spiritual wealth, which in turn engenders exploitation and oppression. So you can see that in Maulana Ali's perspective, injustice is a product of a chain reaction rooted in a basic inversion of spiritual values. It comes from a failure to discern the true nature of things, and therefore inevitably leads to corruption.

## Scribes

Then follows the advise regarding the appointment of scribes. The people who will take his dictations and write his letters - the record keeper. He says
ثُمَّ لاَ يَكُنِ اخْتِيَارُكَ إِيَّاهُمْ عَلَى فِرَاسَتِك وَاسْتِنَامَتِكَ وَحُسْنِ الظَّنِّ مِنْكَ، فَإِنَّ الرِّجَالَ يَتَعَرَّفُونَ لِفِرَاسَاتِ الْوُلاَةِ بِتَصَنُّعِهِمْ وَحُسْنِ خِدْمَتِهِمْ، لَيْسَ وَرَاءَ ذلِكَ مِنَ النَّصِيحَةِ وَالاْمَانَةِ شَيْءٌ 

Your selection of these people should not be based on your understanding, your confidence and your good impression of them; because people create impressions through affectation and personal service, and there is nothing in it which is like well-wishing or trustfulness.
وَلكِنِ اخْتَبِرْهُمْ بِمَا وَلُوا لِلصَّالِحِينَ قَبْلَكَ، فَاعْمِدْ لاِحْسَنِهِمْ كَانَ فِي الْعَامَّةِ أَثَراً، وَأَعْرَفِهِمْ بِالاْمَانَةِ وَجْهاً، فَإِنَّ ذلِكَ دَلِيلٌ عَلَى نَصِيحَتِكَ لله وَلِمَنْ وَلِيتَ أَمْرَهُ 

You should rather test them by what they did under the virtuous people before you. Take a decision in favor of the one who has a good name among the common people, and is the most renowned in his trustworthiness. This will be a proof of your regard for Allah, and for him on whose behalf you have been appointed to this position (namely me).

So Malik is told that he must avoid the temptation to appoint the people who have shown him their best side. A governor must examine their previous records, together with the impression left by them on the common people, and then appoint only those well-known for their trustworthiness. Doing all of this is described as 'proof of your sincerity towards Allah. Why? Because you'll be putting the right people in the right place, thereby enacting justice.

## Traders

Then he talks about merchants and traders. He says:
ثُمَّ اسْتَوْصِ بِالتُّجَّارِ وَذَوِي الصِّنَاعَاتِ، وَأَوْصِ بِهِمْ خَيْراً: الْمُقِيمِ مِنْهُمْ، وَالْمُضْطَرِبِ بِمَالِهِ، وَالْمُتَرَفِّقِ بِبَدَنِهِ، فَإِنَّهُمْ مَوَادُّ الْمَنَافِعِ، وَأَسْبَابُ الْمَرَافِقِ، وَجُلاَّبُهَا مِنَ الْمَباعِدِ وَالْمَطَارِحِ، فِي بَرِّكَ وَبَحْرِكَ، وَسَهْلِكَ وَجَبَلِكَ، وَحَيْثُ لاَ يَلْتَئِمُ النَّاسُ لِمَوَاضِعِهَا، وَلاَ يَجْتَرِئُونَ عَلَيْهَا، فَإِنَّهُمْ سِلْمٌ لاَ تُخَافُ بَائِقَتُهُ، وَصُلْحٌ لاَ تُخْشَى غَائِلَتُهُ 

Now take some advice about traders and industrialists. Give them good counsel whether they are shop-keepers, or traders or physical workers. Because they are sources of profit, and means for the provision of useful entities. They bring them from distant and far-flung areas, through land and sea, plains or mountains, from where people cannot come, and where they do not dare to go. This group is peaceful and there is no fear of revolt from them
وَتَفَقَّدْ أُمُورَهُمْ بِحَضْرَتِكَ وَفِي حَوَاشِي بِلاَدِكَ. وَاعْلَمْ ـ مَعَ ذلِكَ ـ أَنَّ فِي كَثِير مِنْهُمْ ضِيقاً فَاحِشاً، وَشُحّاً قَبِيحاً، وَاحْتِكَاراً لِلْمَنَافِعِ، وَتَحَكُّماً فِي الْبِيَاعَاتِ، وَذلِكَ بَابُ مَضَرَّة لِلْعَامَّةِ، وَعَيْبٌ عَلَى الْوُلاَةِ، فَامْنَعْ مِنَ الاْحْتِكَارِ، فَإِنَّ رَسُولَ اللهِ(صلى الله عليه وآله) مَنَعَ مِنْهُ. وَلْيَكُنِ الْبَيْعُ بَيْعاً سَمْحاً: بِمَوَازِينِ عَدْل، وَأَسْعَار لاَ تُجْحِفُ بِالْفَرِيقَيْنِ مِنَ الْبَائِعِ وَالْمُبْتَاعِ، فَمَنْ قَارَفَ حُكْرَةً بَعْدَ نَهْيِكَ إِيَّاهُ فَنَكِّلْ وَعَاقِبْ فِي غَيْرِ إِسْرَاف. 

Keep an eye on their present affairs, wherever they may be in your area. Know, along with this, that most of them are very narrow-minded, and extremely greedy. They hoard goods for profiteering and fix high prices for goods. This is a source of harm to the people and a blot on the officers in charge. Stop people from hoarding, because the Messenger of Allah (S) has prohibited it. The sale should be smooth, with correct weights and prices, not harmful to either party, the seller or the purchaser; whoever commits hoarding after you prohibit it, give him exemplary punishment, but ensure it is not excessive.

So merchants and craftsmen are then described by Maulana Ali as being those who bring diverse benefits to society, and must therefore be treated well. On the whole they should be left in peace to do their work, but the vices to which they are prone must be kept in check - which are hoarding, miserliness and cheating.

## The Destitute

Maulana Ali now comes to a point in the letter to which he wants Malik to pay special attention. He begins the passage with the exclamation, 'Allah, Allah' to stress its urgency. He says:
ثُمَّ اللهَ اللهَ فِي الطَّبَقَةِ السُّفْلَى مِنَ الَّذِينَ لاَ حِيلَةَ لَهُمْ وَالْمَسَاكِين وَالْـمُحْتَاجِينَ وَأَهْلِ الْبُؤْسَى وَالزَّمْنَى، فإِنَّ فِي هذِهِ الطَّبَقَةِ قَانِعاً وَمُعْتَرّاً، وَاحْفَظْ لله مَا اسْتَحْفَظَكَ مِنْ حَقِّهِ فِيهِمْ، وَاجْعَلْ لَهُمْ قِسمْاً مِنْ بَيْتِ مَالِكَ، فإِنَّ لِلاْقْصَى مِنْهُمْ مِثْلَ الَّذِي لِلاْدْنَى، وَكُلٌّ قَدِ اسْتُرْعِيتَ حَقَّهُ، فَلاَ يَشْغَلنَّكَ عَنْهُمْ بَطَرٌ، فَإِنَّكَ لاَ تُعْذَرُ بِتَضْيِيعِ التَّافِهَ لاِحْكَامِكَ الْكَثِيرَ الْمُهِمَّ. فَلاَ تُشْخِصْ هَمَّكَ عَنْهُمْ، وَلاَ تُصَعِّرْ خَدَّكَ لَهُمْ 

(Fear) Allah and keep Allah in view in respect of the lowest class, comprising those who have few means: the poor, the destitute, the penniless and the crippled. Because in this class are both the needy and the beggars. Take care of them for the sake of Allah, and for the sake of his obligations towards them, for which He has made you responsible. Fix for them a share from the public funds. They are the ones whose rights have been placed in your charge. Therefore, a luxurious life should not keep you away from them. You cannot be excused for ignoring small matters because you were deliberating on bigger problems. Consequently, do not be unmindful of them, nor turn your face from them out of vanity.
وَتَفَقَّدْ أُمُورَ مَنْ لاَ يَصِلُ إِلَيْكَ مِنْهُمْ مِمَّنْ تَقْتَحِمُهُ الْعُيُونُ، وَتَحْقِرُهُ الرِّجَالُ، فَفَرِّغْ لاِولئِكَ ثِقَتَكَ مِنْ أَهْلِ الْخَشْيَةِ وَالتَّوَاضُع، فَلْيَرْفَعْ إِلَيْكَ أُمُورَهُمْ، ثُمَّ اعْمَلْ فِيهِمْ بَالاْعْذَارِ إِلَى اللهِ تَعَالَى يَوْمَ تَلْقَاهُ، فَإِنَّ هؤُلاَءِ مِنْ بَيْنِ الرَّعِيَّةِ أَحْوَجُ إِلَى الاْنصَافِ مِنْ غَيْرِهِمْ، وَكُلٌّ فَأَعْذِرْ إِلَى اللهِ تَعَالَى فِي تَأْدِيَةِ حَقِّهِ إِلَيهِ.

Take care of the affairs of those who do not approach you because they feel contemptible or because they are regarded low. Appoint trusted God-fearing and humble people for them, who should keep you informed about their conditions. Then deal with them with a sense of responsibility to Allah, on the day you will meet Him, because amongst all your subjects, these people are the most deserving of equitable treatment. For others, you should also fulfill their rights, so as to render account to Allah.

The instruction here pertains to those who have no resources at all—the destitute, the crippled, the orphans, the elderly. People who are 'most in need of justice from you. People who should be treated in a manner such that Allah may excuse you on the day you meet Him. He says that all these mentioned, and others who are in need but refuse to beg, should be helped by the governor. He should appoint an officer with the specific task of bringing to light the needs of the most destitute and to provide for them. Then he adds the crucial words :
وَذلِكَ عَلَى الْوُلاَةِ ثَقِيلٌ، وَالْحَقُّ كُلُّهُ ثَقِيلٌ، وَقَدْ يُخَفِّفُهُ اللهُ عَلَى أَقْوَام طَلَبُوا الْعَاقِبَةَ فَصَبَرُوا أَنْفُسَهُمْ، وَوَثِقُوا بِصِدْقِ مَوْعُودِ اللهِ لَهُمْ

This is a heavy burden for the governors, and the fulfillment of all rights is difficult, but Allah makes it easy for those who aspire to the Hereafter, who restrain their souls in patience, and trust in the truth of that which is promised them by Allah.'

Look at what he's saying here. It is the "spiritual component" that makes possible this ideal state that would otherwise be a heavy 'burden'. In other words, divine assistance is assured for those whose attention is not confined to this world alone, but whose aspirations extend to the hereafter. It is only when the hereafter is seen through the lens of this present world; only then a completely "just" attitude emerges towards this world. As for those whose desires or aspirations do not go beyond this world, they will easily fall prey to the easy option of neglecting the destitute; because in the horizontal scales of political evaluation, the costs in pursuing a policy of charity towards the destitute will obviously outweigh the benefits to the ruler.

This is where policies geared exclusively to the benefits of this world reveal their inherent flaws. But when the notion of "truth" in regard to the poor is impregnated with firm faith in Allah's inexorable justice, and infinite compassion, and combined with an aspiration for the Hereafter, an attitude of unflinching fidelity to the needs of the destitute in society will be generated and re-generated ever anew. Such an attitude goes far beyond the conventional notions of charity or generosity. For the person who has perfected this attitude, he will not be susceptible to any contrary suggestion stemming from self-serving pragmatism or individualism, and still less from indifference or willfulness.

With this attitude firmly in place, the next quality mentioned by Maulana Ali, that of patience, flows naturally. For, in the light of a concrete and not merely notional presentiment of the absolute values in question, patience can be indefinitely extended—no longer dependent on personal will alone, but sustained by the empowering grace that flows from heartfelt faith. Maulana Ali completes the above point with reference to those with the right aspirations having confidence in the truth of Allah's promise. If the promise of Allah's reward, of His unfailing justice and His infinite compassion be true, then the motivation behind the governor's effort to extend mercy to his subjects, and to deal justly with the poor will be immeasurably deepened. The task described by Maulana Ali as 'onerous—the obligation to help those who are weak and helpless, those from assisting whom no political benefit may be derived—is transformed into an inescapable duty associated with one's spiritual conviction. This conviction is grounded in the certitude that not only is such generosity and compassion good, right and proper, but that such virtues are in harmony with the true and inalienable substance of ultimate reality. These 'moral' implications o of virtue—are veiled from worldly rulers by their covert prejudices at best, and their overt vices at worst. Such rulers are also oblivious not only to the truth of the divine promise, relating to ultimate felicity, but also to the warnings and threats of retribution for the oppressors, الظالمون. Here, the ظالم can be seen as one who disobeys the divine commandments on justice, and who also, more fundamentally, violates the quality of compassion which lies at the heart of the divine nature.

# Realization of intention

These considerations can be elaborated further by bringing in the altogether crucial issue of intention. Even if the effort of the just man to live according to justice is frustrated by the challenges of worldly life, nonetheless, the right intentions that motivate him—together with his awareness of the immutable divine qualities as models or principles guiding his endeavor, produce a real inward 'taste' of the grace that furnishes the spiritual foundation of his moral life. If Allah's promise is true and one is absolutely certain of this truth, then the impact of that truth on the soul of the sincere believer transforms a personal ethic into an unconditional imperative, a desirable value into an incontrovertible reality. Morality is thus deepened by (and into) spirituality, an existential and all-encompassing mode of being within which the burden of individual 'duty' is not only lightened immeasurably, it is transformed into an accompaniment of one's 'taste' of spiritual reality. One then acts justly out of a sense of gratitude for what one has already received—the 'joy of spiritual certainty', and not only out of hope for something as yet not attained.

For the Quran speaks of the paradisal reward as something already granted to the true believer; the future has already come to pass, for one whose certainty of Paradise is absolute

Abundance [108:1-2]
[ سورۃ ۱۰۸] الکوثر

‏إِنَّآ أَعْطَيْنَكَ ٱلْكَوْثَرَ ‎۱
Verily, We have granted you *Al-Kauthar*; 

‏فَصَلِّ لِرَبِّكَ وَٱنْحَرْ ‎۲
Therefore turn in prayer to your Lord and sacrifice.

Worshiping Allah as a consequence of the certainty of the beatitude to come—and which has thus, in one respect, already come—is well expressed by the following saying of Maulana Ali:  Indeed there is group who worship God out of desire(for something not yet attained); and this is the of the merchants. And there is a group who worship Allah out of fear, and this is the worship of the slaves. And there is a group who worship Allah out of gratitude, and this is the worship of the free*

Furthermore, the just man is liberated from the material consequences of his actions, this, in the measure of the rectitude and sincerity of his intentions, actions being evaluated in the Islamic perspective not according to consequences but intentions. One's intention is to be just, not for the sake of some earthly reward or some tangible consequence in this world, but purely for the sake of justice itself, and this essence or principle of justice is in turn inseparable from the divine nature. For justice is at one with Allah, not simply because that which Allah commands is just; rather, Allah commands just acts precisely because they are just and because this justice is one with His nature* as we noted at the outset: '1 bear witness that He is Justice and He acts justly.' If Allah acts justly as a result of His very nature, which is pure justice, the spiritual man will act justly out of a desire to conform to Allah's nature and not simply out of obedience to Allah's commands, and never out of desire for any earthly reward. As the Quran says:

The Night [92:17-21]
[ سورۃ ۹۲] اللیل

‏وَسَيُجَنَّبُهَا ٱلْأَتْقَى ‎۱۷‏ٱلَّذِى يُؤْتِى مَالَهُۥ يَتَزَكَّىٰ ‎۱۸‏وَمَا لِأَحَدٍ عِندَهُۥ مِن نِّعْمَةٍۢ تُجْزَىٰٓ ‎۱۹‏إِلَّا ٱبْتِغَآءَ وَجْهِ رَبِّهِ ٱلْأَعْلَىٰ ‎۲۰‏وَلَسَوْفَ يَرْضَىٰ ‎۲۱
( 17 ) But the righteous one will avoid it - ( 18 ) He who spends his wealth for increase in self-purification, ( 19 ) And not [giving] for anyone who has [done him] a favor to be rewarded ( 20 ) But only seeking the countenance of his Lord, Most High. ( 21 ) And he is going to be satisfied. 

The same principle is expressed in the description of the righteous, the ابرار. The commentators of Quran agreed that the occasion for the revelation (sabab al-nuzül) of the following verses was when Maulana Ali 'Ali and his family went for three consecutive days without foods-At the end of each day, just when they were about to break their fast, someone more in need than them asked for food. The Quran relates this as follows:

Time or Man [76:8-9]
[ سورۃ ۷۶] الانسان

‏وَيُطْعِمُونَ ٱلطَّعَامَ عَلَىٰ حُبِّهِۦ مِسْكِينًۭا وَيَتِيمًۭا وَأَسِيرًا ‎۸
And they give food in spite of love for it to the needy, the orphan, and the captive,

‏إِنَّمَا نُطْعِمُكُمْ لِوَجْهِ ٱللَّهِ لَا نُرِيدُ مِنكُمْ جَزَآءًۭ وَلَا شُكُورًا ‎۹
[Saying], "We feed you only for the countenance of Allah . We wish not from you reward or gratitude. 

In the very measure that good action is performed for the sake of that absolute goodness which Allah is, the performer of the action is always content, untouched by the blame or praise of others; and neither deflated by any outward adversity that thwarts his good actions, nor inflated by the successful consequences of his good actions. This view of the relation between action and intention helps us appreciate the meaning of Maulana Ali's saying, cited above: 'their hearts are in the Gardens [of Paradise] while their bodies are at work. Again, one observes here the difference between a secular ethic of justice—or one which is devoid of all spiritual or transcendental points of reference—and a spiritual conception of justice. Whereas a secular ethic stands or falls according to the degree of success in realizing outwardly its ideal of justice, a spiritual conception already sees the fulfillment of its ideal in the sincere intention to be just. For the fulfillment of a spiritual ideal of justice essentially requires an unyielding struggle to achieve it, failing which the intention is shown to be lacking sincerity. Without associated action, aspirations and intentions are lacking in depth and remain purely theoretical. To hold an intention with total sincerity strictly requires striving diligently and tirelessly in pursuit of its realization. Thus, however formidable the obstacles, however compelling the arguments of political pragmatism maybe, however much one's strenuous efforts are brought to nothing due to unavoidable exigencies of outward life, the just man never despairs; he never abandons the effort to achieve justice, precisely because the achievement of justice in this world is not the sum total of his aims. The just person knows that the intention is already, in an invisible but spiritually palpable way, its own fulfillment; and he knows, with a sense of sober realism, that the outward realization of all intentions depends upon circumstances beyond his control, and ultimately on the grace of Allah.

Now, while this may seem on the surface to condone the failure to achieve justice in society and is thus exploitable as a subterfuge by which actual justice is legitimated by appealing to good but thwarted intentions, the very opposite, in fact, is the case. Far from implying any lack of resolve on the part of one motivated by a sacred conception of justice, the efforts of such a person will, on the contrary, be as ceaseless and unyielding as the reward in which one fervently believes is eternal and unfailing. Such efforts will also be sustained by the consciousness of the subtle continuity between just intentions and the divine quality of justice and thereby the divine nature itself. An inalienable sense of the sacred, then, sustains the just man in his pursuit of justice.

One is not arguing that all secular or atheistic conceptions of justice are devoid of all value; only that, in comparison with a sacred conception of justice, they will lack those immeasurable and inexhaustible spiritual resources that are nourished by sincere faith. With men this is impossible, but with Allah all things are possible', as Jesus says, according to the Gospel. Put into the present context, one might formulate this principle as follows: When justice is conceived in isolation from spiritual principles, personal idealism is acutely sensitive to outward contingencies, and all too likely to disintegrate under the pressure of outward difficulties and disappointments; in a sacred conception of justice, by contrast, divine grace supports the incorruptible ideal of justice, which is thus held high above the frailties and vicissitudes to which the human soul and human society are both subject. To the extent that one's ideals are rooted in spiritual knowledge, then, one's susceptibility to disappointment and despair in the face of difficulties is diminished.

The salience of this ideal of justice is clearly to be seen in Maulana Ali's own experience as caliph: a continuous series of bloody conflicts, against foes that professed Islam, prevented him outwardly from putting into effect many of the principles and policies that flowed from his conception of truth and justice—but none of this resulted in dejection, despair or cynicism, or in even a slight diminution in his efforts to achieve justice whenever and wherever possible, as is amply demonstrated in the nature of his own administration, and in the stream of letters and counsels to his officers and governors, including the one now being considered. Such despair is almost inconceivable for one whose inner life is determined by the conviction that 'No act is negligible if it is accompanied by piety. 'For such a person, despair is excluded by piety, in the deepest sense of the term, that is, the permanent awareness of divine Reality, for this degree of piety is the مخرج or 'way out' from the darkness of all injustices on earth. As Maulana Ali says, 'He who has pious awe towards Allah, for him Allah establishes a way out of tribulation, and a light out of darkness (من یتَّقِ اللہُ مخرجاً من الفتن و نوراً من الظلم).

# THE VIRTUE OF WORSHIP

We now come to what is arguably the lynchpin of the entire epistle, the most important means by which the sacred substance of all the virtues is assimilated by the ruler, namely, prayer. We saw at the beginning of the letter how the

Maulana Ali began with a counsel to observe the obligatory prayers. At this point, after stressing the need to perform all administrative tasks in their appropriate times, Maulana Ali instructs Mälik to set apart the 'most excellent' of his

available time for 'what is between you and Allah',ÅQ3 that is, acts of worship and devotion. Malik is urged to pay special attention to the obligatory prayers as the means by which he 'purifies' his religion for Allah or renders sincere his

devotion to Allah. Now, this instruction is not to be taken only as an exhortation to perform the formal prayers; it must also, above all, be understood in the light of the deeper meaning of 'purifying one's religion for Allah', that is,

purifying the substance of the relationship betneen the soul and Allah, for it is the depth of this relationship that vill determine the quality of all human virtue. As Maulana Ali put it, in the saying noted above: 'Know that pur every act

is dependent upon your prayer.AQ4 It is not only the formal, physical and værbal accomplishment of the prayer that matters, but the inward quality that accompanies it and enlivens it from within. 105 The sincerity, intensity and

frequency of prayer are essential to its efficacy. Maulana Ali continues: 'Give unto Allah of your vital energv in your nights and your days, and perform fully that by which you draw near to Allah, doing so perfectly, without becoming

dull or deficient, taking your body to its limits (bälighan min badanika mä balagha).'

All the stress here should be on the •fiord taqarrub, 'drawing near'. One attains the divine nearness most directly through deep devotion, offered up alone, in the dead of the night, with all one's heart.ÅQ6 This nearness being

realized, all of one's other actions in the world are imbued with something of the spiritual quality gained through the divine proximity. It is in this manner that all action can be assimilated to 'devotion' in this wider sense, all

outward actions being truly governed bythe sincere intention to give oneself to Allah. If one's inner life is dominated by prayer, then prayer comes to penetrate one's outer life also. Hence, as Maulana Ali said, just after telling Malik to

set apart the best of his times for Allah, 'all times Cand actions performed therein) are for Allah if the intention underlying them is good and if your subjects derive security as a consequence.' In other words, all the actions of the ruler

will partake ofthe sacred, in the measure that his devotion truly enters into the source of the sacred. Then one can envisage an overflow, as it were, from the fountain of obligatory and voluntary prayer, into the outward domain of

action. This subtle truth is beautifully expressed in the famous hadith qudsi of the Prophet, often referred to as the hadith of taqarrub:

My near to Me through nothing I love more than that have made obligatory for him. ceases to draw near to through until And when I love him, I am his hearing by which he hears, his sight by he sees, his hand

which he grasps, and his foot by v.'hich he

To 'see' and act through Allah can be understood, at one level at least, to mean seeing things as they really are, and then acting upon that vision; in other words, it implies perfect justice, putting each thing in its proper place. It is

important to note in the above hadith qudsi the distinction between the obligatory and the supererogatory acts of devotion—both are means of 'drawing near' but it is primarily through the latter that the slave comes to perceive and

act through the divine.

It is also important to note Maulana Ali's phrase, 'taking your body to its limits'. Strenuous effort is called for here, it is far from sufficient to perform the prayers and devotions in a mechanical or perfunctory manner. 'He who prays

without making an effort,' says Maulana Ali, 'is like one who shoots arrows without a bow.'ÅQÅ As noted earlier, the sincerity (ikhläs) demanded by Maulana Ali's conception of the oneness of Allah calls for the integration of the soul, and it

is in prayer that the deepest dimensions of one's soul are expressed, cultivated and integrated. Hence, the prayer must be accomplished not just with one's tongue or one's body, but with all that one is.

Elsewhere, Maulana Ali 'Ali refers to the key (supererogatory) practice by which this spiritual station of 'nearness' is attained. He does this in a commentary upon the following words of a Qur'änic verse:

men whom neither

commerce nor trade diverts from the remembrance of Allah' (24: 36).

Truly Gcd has made the remembrance (al-dhzl:r) a polish for the hearts, by which they hear after suffering from deafness, and see after being blind There been of Allah with whom He held intimate discourse through their thoughts and spoke with them through the

essence of their intellects. They diffused illumination through the av.zkened light in their hearing and their seeing and their hearts, calling unto the remembrance of the days of

The importance of the dhikr, both as a principle of awareness of ultimate reality and as a concrete means of attaining this awareness is clearly of the utmost importance in Maulana Ali's perspective. It is necessary- to stress here that

Maulana Ali implies, by the word dhikr, a methodic practice of invoking a divine Name, as well as a principle of permanent awareness of Allah embracing all modes of worship, meditation and reflection. It is to be understood not only

in the sense of being aware of Allah, or remembering Allah, but also as an intense mode of prolonged, prayerful meditation on the ultimate reality, represented both symbolically and sacramentally by one or more of the divine Names.

(This aspect of the remembrance will be addressed more fully in the follovv-ing chapter.)

The relationship between the remembrance of Allah and the cultivation ofjustice is made clear in this sermon. For Maulana Ali describes those who are illuminated by the dhikr as follows:

Truly there are people Thobelong to the dhtkr, they adopted it in place of the world, such that 'neither ammerc-e nor trade' distracts them from it; they spend the days of their in it they instruct people about justice and themsehes are steadfast therein.æ

It is those souls who truly 'belong to the dhikr' that are not only 'steadfast' in justice, but also able to 'instruct people about justice'; putting first things first, they are able to show how to put all things in their proper place. Fervent,

lived spirituality is therefore not o pposed to dedicated activity in the world, but infuses that activity with an ethical depth that springs from direct co ntact with the divine , a direct contact articulated by prayer in general and by the

dhikr in particular.

Just as earlier it was seen that the sincerity of a good intention was proven by the effort to realize it, so it is clear now that, in regard to prayer, its sincerity is likewise proven and rendered efficacious through sustained effort to

prolong and intensifyit, 'taking your body to its limits' æ V,hat this has to do with justice should be clear: if justice is grasped as a quality rooted in the Real (al-äaqq), then one's nearness to the Real cannot but draw one nearer to

justice and to all positive qualities, spiritual, intellectual and moral. Thus, the relationship between worship and justice must be firmly established ifthe conception of justice is to be qualified as 'sacred'. From this perspective,

justice is both cause and consequence of worship. On one hand, justice requires us to put everything in its place and, thus, Allah must be given His due, which entails, sacramentally, worship and prayer; on the other hand, justice

emerges, ethically, as a consequence of this worship, as do all the virtues, in the measure of the 'nearness' granted by Allah to His worshipper, and in accordance with the radiant repercussions of that nearness.

Gazing on the Good

We have made occasional references above to some similarities betneen principles expressed in Maulana Ali's letter and those discussed in Plato's The Republic. This chapter can be brought to a close by proposing that what we are

given by Maulana Ali in his stress on prayer—together with its essence, the remembrance of Allah—is a devotional principle which illuminates and, in a sense, sublimates the philosophical position of Plato, and might be seen as

rendering explicit in spiritual terms what is implied in Plato's pedagogy". After discussing the question of justice at great length, Socrates comes, in Chapter 23 of The Republic, to address the crux of the matter: the nature of the

Sovereign Good, which most concerns the ideal ruler, the 'guardian', whose moral and intellectual education is at the heart of the practical dimension of the dialogue:

the highest obje:t of knouledgeis the essential nature of the Good, from vhich everything that is gcod and right derives its value for us. I need not tell that, vhthout that knovdedge to know evecthing Ase, how-eer v.'ell, vould be of no value to us

But Socrates does not respond to the entreaties of Glaucon to describe the nature of this Good, offering instead to speak of its 'offspring'. This leads him to make an analogv between the Good and the Sun: what the Sun is to

visibility, the Good is to intelligibility and being. He proceeds vith the famous allegory ofthe cave and then describes the educational programme that would turn the attention of his prospective rulers (the guardians) from the

norld of shadows to the norld of real objects, a programme comprising mathematics, arithmetic, geometry, solid geometry, astronomy, harmonics and dialectic. Having successfully passed through all stages of their education,

'when they are fifty, those who have come safely through and proved the best at all points in action and in study must be brought at last to the goal. They must lift up the eye of the soul to gaze on that which sheds light on all things;

and when they have seen the Goo d itself, take it as a pattern for the right ordering of the state and of the in dividual, themselves included. But Plato do es no t anywhere state what this 'gazing' actu ally means, nor how it is to be

achieved, by what means, intellective, contemplative or methodic.

At an important point in The Republic, Glaucon presses Socrates on the definition ofthe Sovereign Good: 'But, Socrates, what is your account of the Good? Is it knowledge, or pleasure, or something else?' Socrates refuses to give

an account, saying, after some further discussions, 'I am afraid it is beyond my povv•ers; with the best will in the world I should only disgrace myself and be laughed at.'ÅÅ-4 It is interesting to note in this connection that Dhi 'lib al-

Yamäni, very much after the manner of Glaucon, provocatively asked Maulana Ali 'Ali whether he had seen the object of his devotion. To this Maulana Ali replied: '1 nould not be worshipping a lord whom I have not seen.' Dhi 'lib then

asked, 'O Commander of the Faithful! How did you see Him?' Maulana Ali replied with the important sentence that functions as one of the leitmotifs of our book: 'O Dhi'lib! Eyes see Him not through sight's observation, but hearts

see Him through the verities of faith.

We are reminded here of the 'polishing' of the heart effected by remembrance, and this very word evokes the essence of Plato's approach to knowledge as 'recollection', anamnesisæf But, as noted above, Plato does not, in any of

his dialogues, make a direct reference to the devotional, methodic or contemplative counterpart to his educational, ethical and intellective endeavour. There is no explicitly postulated link between 'remembrance' as regards the

forms/ideas' which engender true knowledge, and the 'gazing upon the Good', the contemplation of 'that which sheds light on all things'; in other words, not just knowing,/remembering the forms', but 'the Bestower of Forms',

which in Islam is referred to by the divine Name, al-Mu;awwir. There is speculation as to whether there was any spiritual method in Plato's teaching, and if there was, why he did not divulge itEZ One answer can be gauged from a

statement in Plato's famous Seventh I_.etter, where he denies that any exposition can be given of the true reality of philosophy, and points instead to the 'disciplined way oflife' and rigorous 'daily routine' which the subject demands

if philosophy in the deepest sense is to be realized. 'No treatise by me concerning it exists or ever will exist. It is not something that can be put into words like other branches of learning; only after long partnership in a common life

devoted to this very thing does truth flash upon the soul, like a flame kindled by a leaping spark, and once it is born there, it nourishes itself thereafteru

This brings us back to the importance of active spirituality for the practice of authentic virtue. If the Real—indefinable in its essence—is the foundation of all good, the means of assimilating the Real existentially—and not just

comprehending it mentally—cannot be disdained. Plato's philosophical approach to the Good can be augmented and deepened by an appreciation of Maulana Ali 'Ali's means of 'drawing near' to the Real, for in Maulana Ali's perspective,

intellectual comprehension and spiritual devotion are inseparable and complementary. Integral, deep-rooted justice emerges as one of the fruits ofthis 'drawing near', just as, for Plato, gazing on 'that which sheds light on all things

.. the Good itself results in the capacity to take the Good 'as a pattern for the right ordering of the state and of the individual'.

In this chapter we have attempted to sketch the outline.s of a spiritual approach to the theme of justice, such as this presents itself in, principally, the letter of Maulana Ali to Malik al-Ashtar. We are fully aware that we have only

skimmed the surface of this important subject, but hope nonetheless to have at least indicated that Maulana Ali's illumination of the principle of justice, although addressed in the first instance to a prospective governor of Eypt in the

first century of the Islamic era, is by no means outmoded. Whether one is operating in an avowedly Islamic or an overtly secular environment, whether the rule be autocratic or democratic, whether political institutions be strong or

neak: where'.er there are individuals vested vith power and authority—and by that very token, subject to the temptations offered by power—the approach to justice articulated in Maulana Ali's ethos will not be irrelevant. For this

ethos does not presuppose any particular form of government, it engages the universal theme of moral responsibility, but doing so in terms of that which transcends the level of morality, offering us insights derived from a direct

vision of the ultimate spiritual realities. The principles which are contemplated thanks to this •vision surpass the domain of politics, and, for that very- reason, are capable of penetrating deeply and refashioning, from above, the

moral substance of political consciousness. So, although Maulana Ali's perspective is particularly pertinent to the interface between spirituality, morality and governance within Islamic discourse and praxis, its relevance extends to all

contexts wherein the sense of the sacred plays a part in the articulation of moral sensibility and the cultivation of political responsibility.

We also hope to have demonstrated here that Maulana Ali's letters and discourses are a vast and largely untapped source of inspired ideas that can illuminate the role of spiritual principles in formulating ethical values and

determining the quality of political practice. In particular, it should be clear that an integral 'Islamic' approach to politics and society must take into account the requirements and the fruits of 'worship'—understanding this •fiord in

its widest sense. Sincere worship transforms the notion of the Absolute into a concrete reality that imparts to the soul an existential impetus and a spiritual resource which buttresses, stabilizes and deepens one's moral life. What is

derived from our consciousness of the divine Reality is not so much a philosophy of ethics as a 'spiritual morality', an existential orientation that is both the consequence of a lived spirituality and the cause of continuous, dynamic

and ever-deepening realization of spiritual truth, the Real as such, al-äaqq. This means that the haqq or right due to each person or group will be given, according to the principle of justice, 'putting each thing in its proper place'.

Virtue, in such a perspective, is a form of 'ethical realism', understanding by 'realism' here an assimilation of spiritual reality; and morally correct behaviour translates that realism into action on all planes, individual, social and

political.

If it be objected that this perspective is hopelessly utopian, the harsh exigencies of politics and the sublime ideals of spirituality being poles apart, we would respond: if no effort is made to comprehend the deeper aspects of

Islamic piety and spirituality, the result of any ostensibly 'Islamic' discourse—whether in politics, socioloy, ethics or any other field—vill be reduced to an outer shell, deprived of the inner life that gives it all its meaning and, more

importantly, its transformative power. For Islamic concepts to be grasped aright they must, on pain of abstraction, be assimilated not simply by the mind but by the heart. As Maulana Ali stresses, it is the heart—the inmost point of

one's consciousness—that 'sees' the true nature ofthings. Without this vision of Allah 'as if one could see Him', there is no i&sän in the ethico-spiritual sense discussed earlier, and without itsän, the very integrity of the Islamic way of

life is fatally undermined, as has been amply proven by a tiny minority of Muslims in our times who betray the highest ideals of the Islamic ethical tradition by resorting to unbridled violence in its name. Without itsän, religion is

reduced to a position of acute vulnerability; it becomes transformed into an ideoloy, determined by individual passion and subject to political manipulation. It is no surprise to find that those most prone to terrorism in the name of

Islam have arisen out of currents most hostile to the itsänt or mystical tradition.

This leads us back to the devotional practices that are all too often regarded as the preserve of a minority—seen as mystical or misguided, depending on one's standpoint—instead of being grasped as a Allah-given means of

reducing the gap betneen what one aspires to and what one is; of moving from the actual to the ideal; of making concrete what otherwise nould remain abstract; of living that which is thought about; and of rendering existentially

galvanizing what otheraise might perhaps only be theoretically compelling. V,hile the principles established here are universal in their import and applicability, it would be wrong to try and 'de-contextualize' the perspective of the

Maulana Ali, if this means ignoring—as so much unnecessary 'superstructure'—his repeated, insistent and uncompromising emphasis upon the imperative of prayer. The principle of justice requires that all the elements of one's life

devotion, worship, ethics, action—be bound together in a harmonious, indissoluble and interconnected whole, in accordance with the ideal of tawhid. There can be no question of detaching the domain of the virtues from that of

spirituality, for in Maulana Ali's perspective, the only true iirtue is one that is rooted in, sustained by and oriented towards the divine Reality. Thus, one must conclude that justice, together with all the other virtues, can only be

brought to fruition in a soul—and in a society—that is permeated and penetrated by spirituality, the essence of which is the principle and the practice of the remembrance of Allah. In other words, it is the remembrance of Allah that, in

the last analysis, determines the quality of human virtue. It is to the spiritual realization intended and cultivated by this remembrance that the following chapter is addressed.
